If u is nonempty and u≠m, encoding leaves u unchanged and extension appends z, giving the same equality. At u=m, however, the left side is [z] and the right side is m++[z], with lengths one and two. Consequently
  X(e(u),z)=e(u++[z]) ⇔ u≠m.

On raw forms different from m, encoding is injective. Two nonempty forms are unchanged; if one is empty, equality of the encoded outputs forces the other to be m, excluded by the premise. If the carrier contains no literal marker label, containment of raw selections excludes m from every raw form. Encoding therefore preserves distinctness on that domain.

Mapping e over the raw successor equation and applying the commutation identity yields
  map(e,S(C++[z]))=map(e,S(C)) ++ map(v↦X(v,z),map(e,S(C))).
When z∉C and z≠"empty-One", it occurs in neither an ordinary encoded selection nor the encoded empty trace; hence the recurrence's original freshness guard passes. With the original nonempty/distinct carrier guard, this establishes the exact successful API recurrence. The executable guard itself does not exclude the marker: on C=["empty-One"], S(C)=[[],m] and map(e,S(C))=[m,m], exactly as in V3. The unrestricted correspondence is instead
  v∈map(e,S(C)) ⇔ ∃u, H(C,u) and e(u)=v.
It retains both raw derivations even when their displayed encodings coincide. No marker restriction is added to the source operation.


30. Arrangement successor partition and provenance

For every nonempty distinct finite carrier and every fresh label, a form belongs to the original successor arrangement family if and only if it is obtained by inserting the fresh label between a prefix and suffix of a prior generated arrangement. The proof removes the fresh label from the successor carrier, identifies the remaining form through permutation completeness, and reconstructs every inserted form through the same original generator. The prefix and suffix are uniquely determined: freshness prevents the inserted label from being mistaken for any prior occurrence. Thus the successor family is exhausted by disjoint insertion-position classes with retained prior construction provenance. This result is uniform over finite carrier size and leaves the source-ordered enumeration unchanged.

Let C be nonempty and distinct, and z∉C. Removal distributes over concatenation, R(U++V,z)=R(U,z)++R(V,z); therefore R(C++[z],z)=C. If v∈A(C++[z]), permutation preservation places z in v. Splitting at that occurrence gives v=B++z::D. Moving z past B gives B++z::D∼z::(B++D). Removal of z from the successor permutation now yields B++D∼C, so completeness supplies B++D∈A(C).

Conversely, if B++D∈A(C), then B++D∼C. Prepending z and moving it to the indicated position gives B++z::D∼C++[z]. Successor completeness therefore generates that inserted form. Hence
  v∈A(C++[z]) ⇔ ∃B,D, B++D∈A(C) and v=B++z::D.
For uniqueness, suppose B++z::D=B′++z::D′. Neither prefix contains z, because each belongs to a prior arrangement of C. If one prefix is empty and the other is not, equality of the first labels forces z into the nonempty prefix, a contradiction. Otherwise equality of first labels reduces to the same problem on the tails. Induction gives B=B′ and D=D′, retaining the unique prior arrangement and insertion position.


31. Arrangement trace accounting and arithmetic linkage

The count of the arrangement family is obtained directly from its ordered held-choice branches. A singleton carrier generates one arrangement. At each larger carrier, the length of the complete generated trace equals the ordered sum of the generated remainder-family lengths; prepending the held label preserves each branch length. This equation is derived from the enumerated trace and does not replace its generation with a closed-form answer.

For every admitted carrier of at least two labels, the first held-choice branch and the concatenation of the remaining branches are both nonempty and disjoint. Applying the existing exact-arithmetic disjoint junction to these actual traces succeeds under its original guards and returns the original complete arrangement family. This connects combinatorial branch accounting directly to the translated arithmetic operation while preserving source order and every generated form.

For a supplied carrier C of at least two labels, define its held branch B_x=map(u↦x::u,A(R(C,x))). The original recursion states A(C)=concat_(x∈C) B_x in carrier order. Mapping preserves length and concatenation adds lengths, so
  |A(C)|=Σ_(x∈C) |B_x|=Σ_(x∈C) |A(R(C,x))|,
  |A([x])|=1.
The sum is the finite ordered fold of actual branch lengths, with host zero only as the empty accumulator; the arrangement family is still generated in full.

For C=first::second::rest with distinct labels, each relevant remainder is a nonempty distinct carrier. Its identity permutation is generated by completeness, proving A(R(C,x)) nonempty. Thus B_first and the concatenation of the remaining branches are nonempty. An intersection would equate first::u with x::v for some x in second::rest, forcing first=x and contradicting distinctness. The arithmetic overlap test consequently returns false, and both nonempty guards pass. Its exact operation therefore gives
  disjointJunction(B_first, concat_(x∈second::rest) B_x)=ok(A(C)).
This equality retains every source-ordered branch and connects the count to the actual arithmetic junction.


32. Complete encoded selection output

For every successful call to the original selections operation, output membership is equivalent to the existence of a raw absent/held construction whose empty-form encoding equals the returned form. This equivalence covers the full string-label input domain of the source API. Every such output is a positive trace and retains the structural empty One, derived by choosing the absent branch at each carrier extension.

If the carrier omits the literal empty-One label, each encoded result has a unique raw-form preimage and the output is duplicate-free. These uniqueness conclusions retain their explicit premise; the full-domain membership and completeness theorem requires no such exclusion. The preimage result identifies raw forms, without asserting equality of proof terms or erasing the source construction order.

Let E(C)=map(e,S(C)), using the raw held-choice family and encoding already defined. The list-map membership rule gives
  v∈E(C) ⇔ ∃u, u∈S(C) and e(u)=v.
Substituting u∈S(C)⇔H(C,u) proves the stated full-domain correspondence. The base H([],[]) extends to H(C,[]) by taking the absent constructor at every successor. Since e([])=m, the empty-One trace m belongs to E(C); therefore E(C) is nonempty. Each encoded form is itself nonempty: the empty raw form becomes the singleton marker, and every other raw form is retained.

If the carrier contains no marker label, suppose H(C,u), H(C,w) and e(u)=v=e(w). Both raw forms avoid m by containment, and encoding injectivity gives w=u. This establishes a unique raw-form preimage, while distinctness of the raw family yields distinctness of E(C). For an accepted call selections(C)=ok(L), the original guard supplies nonempty/distinct C and the implementation supplies L=E(C). Substitution yields the membership and positivity statements for the actual returned family, with encoded uniqueness requiring only its separately stated marker-absence premise.


33. Discrete collection integration

Every arrangement family over a nonempty distinct carrier satisfies the original discrete generated-collection operation and has its finite singleton/successor construction trace. Each held-choice branch is a nonempty, duplicate-free subset of the complete family, so the original held-selection operation accepts it and retains its exact tuple representation.

The encoded selection family likewise has a generated-collection construction trace when the carrier is distinct and omits the literal empty-One label. The concrete composition on the singleton literal-marker carrier is also established: selections returns two equal encoded forms, and generated_collection rejects that result through its existing duplicate guard. The translated operations preserve this source behavior. No unconditional collection-admission conclusion is inferred for all encoded selections.

Write G(L) for the generated-collection operation. Its source guard requires L nonempty and its member forms distinct. For an admitted arrangement carrier C, the identity permutation C belongs to A(C), so A(C) is nonempty; Section 26 supplies its distinctness. Consequently
  G(A(C))=ok(A(C)).
The existing collection construction theorem then supplies CollectionTrace(A(C)): a nonempty distinct family is built from its first singleton by appending each remaining fresh form, preserving source order.

For the held branch B_x, membership in the branch supplies membership in A(C) through the exact concatenation equation. Its recursive suffix family is nonempty and distinct, and prepending x is injective, so B_x has both properties. Thus every held-selection guard passes and
  heldSelection(A(C),B_x)=ok(collectionValue(B_x)).
For E(C), Section 32 supplies nonemptiness and, when the literal marker is absent, distinctness. Hence G(E(C))=ok(E(C)) and its CollectionTrace follows in the same way. At the literal-marker singleton, however, E(["empty-One"])=[m,m]; the duplicate guard rejects this exact family. This is the original composition of the two operations, without a new restriction on the selections input domain.


34. Ordered selection provenance

A raw form is generated by the absent/held recurrence if and only if it is an order-preserving subsequence of the carrier. Both directions are established constructively: every recurrence step preserves source order, while every ordered choice can be built by retaining or skipping carrier positions. Encoding this relation gives the exact membership characterization of the returned selection family, including the empty-One conversion.

Every held successor is the existing Fold word-append operation on the prior raw form. Its return operation recovers that exact prior form, and its last-label operation recovers the appended label. This retains the local construction provenance of every held step. The word-operation identity does not extend the fixed binary Fold support to arbitrary string labels.

Write u⊑C for an order-preserving sublist. The base []⊑[] matches the base held-choice constructor. If u⊑C, skipping a new final label gives u⊑C++[x], while retaining it gives u++[x]⊑C++[x]. Induction on H(C,u) therefore proves H(C,u)⇒u⊑C. For the converse, sublist construction either skips a leading carrier label or retains that label at the front of both lists. Prefixing an absent or held choice is itself derived by induction on the existing held-choice construction. These two prefix constructions translate the sublist induction into H(C,u). Thus
  u∈S(C) ⇔ H(C,u) ⇔ u⊑C,
  v∈E(C) ⇔ ∃u, u⊑C and e(u)=v.
For each held successor, appendLabel(u,x)=u++[x]. The established word identities give
  returnWord(appendLabel(u,x))=some(u),
  lastLabel(appendLabel(u,x))=some(x).
The held-choice successor rule simultaneously places this appended word in S(C++[x]). These equalities recover the exact local predecessor and label without changing the support of the binary Fold grammar.


35. Raw choices as discrete held selections

Each raw selection of a distinct carrier retains distinct labels in carrier order. Representing each label by its singleton form gives a generated discrete carrier collection. Under the same representation, every raw selection is accepted by the original discrete held-selection operation: a nonempty choice returns its exact collection tuple, while an empty choice returns the original structural empty-One form. The proof uses retained membership and distinctness and requires no exclusion of the literal empty-One label. This relation concerns raw choices and explicitly represented member forms; it does not identify the differently nested encoded outputs of the two source functions.

For any sublist u⊑C, membership is retained: x∈u⇒x∈C. Induction on the sublist constructors also preserves distinctness. A skipped carrier head leaves the induction result unchanged; a retained head was absent from the carrier tail and hence from its sublist, so it remains fresh in the selected trace. Thus distinct(C) and u∈S(C) imply distinct(u).

Define the singleton representation J(C)=map(x↦[x],C). It is injective on labels because [x]=[y] implies x=y. Therefore nonempty distinct C yields nonempty distinct J(C), and generatedCollection(J(C))=ok(J(C)). For u∈S(C), retained membership gives J(u)⊆J(C), and injectivity gives distinct(J(u)). Substitution in the original held-selection guards yields
  heldSelection(J(C),J(u))=
    ok(formValue(m)) when u=[],
    ok(collectionValue(J(u))) when u≠[].
The empty branch is the original empty-One case; the nonempty branch retains the nested singleton forms. All of these statements permit the literal marker as a carrier label because the map x↦[x] remains injective there.


36. Finite algebraic operation tables

The operation-table constructor retains the registered carrier and ordered input/output rows. It first requires a nonempty distinct carrier, then checks that row count equals the complete ordered pair support and that the input sets coincide, and finally checks every result belongs to the carrier. These are the original source checks in their original order. Accepted tables preserve their data, cover exactly the carrier pair support, and have carrier-closed results.

Application filters the retained rows by the requested ordered input pair and returns a result only when exactly one row matches. Empty or multiple matches retain the source error. Fresh execution matches 526 constructor cases and 2630 lookup cases, including complete two-member operations in every row order and malformed inputs. General single-valuedness and total lookup follow from the constructor conditions as established below; the remaining algebraic property derivations are tracked separately.

For the retained carrier C, define the ordered support P(C)=concat_(a∈C)[(a,b):b∈C]. For the row list R, let K(R) project each row (a,b,c) to its input pair (a,b). The constructor evaluates, in order,
  C≠[] and distinct(C),
  |R|=|P(C)| and (∀p, p∈K(R) ⇔ p∈P(C)),
  ∀(a,b,c)∈R, c∈C.
The second line expresses the actual two-direction membership test together with the length test; it does not assume row-input uniqueness. Passing these checks returns exactly the retained pair of lists (C,R), without sorting or replacing the supplied rows.

For a requested pair (a,b), define M_R(a,b)=map((x,y,z)↦z,filter((x,y,z)↦x=a and y=b,R)). Application returns ok(c) when M_R(a,b)=[c], and the original error when the list is empty or has at least two entries. Constructor closure supplies carrier membership for every output of a matching retained row. The next section derives singleton matching from the constructor's actual coverage and length checks.


37. Complete single-valued operation support

The constructor’s input coverage and row cardinality jointly force the projected input rows to be a permutation of the complete ordered pair support. This follows constructively for arbitrary finite size: locate the first support element in the proposed rows, remove that occurrence, and retain coverage and equal length for the remaining support. Since the carrier is distinct, the complete pair support is distinct, hence so is the projected row-input list.

Consequently, for every pair of carrier members the actual matching-row filter contains exactly one row. The translated apply operation returns its result, and the constructor’s closure check proves that result remains in the registered carrier. No uniqueness assumption is added to the source guards; it is derived from their original coverage and cardinality conditions.

The finite coverage lemma is: if a list P has distinct members, every member of P occurs in Q, and |P|=|Q|, then P∼Q. For P=[], length equality forces Q=[]. For P=x::P′, coverage splits Q=B++x::D. Distinctness gives x∉P′. Each y∈P′ still occurs in B++D: its supplied occurrence in Q cannot be the removed x. Cancelling the one removed position from the length equality gives |P′|=|B++D|. Induction supplies P′∼B++D, and restoring x and moving it across B gives x::P′∼Q.

For distinct C, P(C) is distinct: within each fixed first coordinate, (a,b)=(a,b′) implies b=b′; between distinct first coordinates, pair equality is impossible. The constructor's coverage and count conditions therefore instantiate the lemma to give
  P(C)∼K(R), hence distinct(K(R)).
For a,b∈C, the pair (a,b) belongs to P(C) and thus K(R). Filter R by this pair. Existence guarantees a matching row. Projected-input distinctness excludes every second matching row, so the actual filter is [(a,b,c)] for one retained row. Mapping to results gives M_R(a,b)=[c]. Application consequently returns ok(c), and the original closure check gives c∈C. Totality and single-valuedness follow from the original guards for arbitrary admitted finite carrier size.


38. Exhaustive identity and association

The identity test first checks that the proposed identity belongs to the carrier, then evaluates left and right preservation for each carrier member in source order. Its successful result is equivalent to both applications returning that member for every carrier member. The conjunction preserves source short-circuiting and lookup exceptions.

The associativity test traverses every ordered carrier triple. For each triple it evaluates the left composite before the right composite in the original order. On accepted operation tables, successful exhaustive checking is equivalent to equality of the two actual composed results for every triple. Totality and closure of the constructor supply the intermediate operation results, so no associativity law is assumed. Fresh execution agrees on 156 property calls over 39 table representations, including every two-member binary operation in two orders and malformed diagnostic representations.

Let μ(a,b) denote the successful result of applyOperation on an accepted table with carrier C. Section 37 establishes μ(a,b)∈C for a,b∈C. The checked traversal has the equations allChecked(p,[])=ok(true); on x::X it evaluates p(x), continues on X only after ok(true), returns ok(false) after false, and propagates an error. Induction on X proves
  allChecked(p,X)=ok(true) ⇔ ∀x∈X, p(x)=ok(true).
For an identity cell, success of the left comparison forces μ(e,a)=a; only then is the right comparison evaluated, and success forces μ(a,e)=a. Combining this equivalence with the initial membership guard and traversal gives
  hasIdentity(e)=ok(true) ⇔ e∈C and ∀a∈C, μ(e,a)=a=μ(a,e).
For association, the evaluated sequence is u=μ(a,b), l=μ(u,c), v=μ(b,c), r=μ(a,v). Closure supplies u,v∈C, so all four lookups exist. The cell is true exactly when l=r. Applying the traversal equivalence at each of the three nested carrier lists yields
  isAssociative=ok(true) ⇔ ∀a,b,c∈C, μ(μ(a,b),c)=μ(a,μ(b,c)).
This derives the meaning of exhaustive checking; it does not assert that every accepted table is associative.


39. Held return mates and commutation

The return-mate operation retains the source identity precheck. For each carrier member it exhausts candidate mates in carrier order and tests return to identity on both sides. Exactly one matching candidate is required; zero or multiple matches return None, while lookup exceptions propagate. Successful candidate filtering retains precisely those carrier members satisfying both return equations. Successful mate-row construction preserves the carrier order and provides a singleton candidate search for each returned row.

Commutation is evaluated on every ordered carrier pair, comparing both operation results with the original left-before-right evaluation. Its successful result is equivalent to all pair comparisons passing, and each comparison is equivalent to equality of the actual results. Fresh execution matches 156 return-mate and commutation calls across 39 table representations, covering all two-member operations in two orders and malformed diagnostics.

For a proposed identity e and an item a, define the successful candidate list K_e(a) by checked filtering of C. A candidate b is retained precisely when both ordered operations return e. Induction on the filter gives
  b∈K_e(a) ⇔ b∈C and applyOperation(a,b)=ok(e) and applyOperation(b,a)=ok(e).
The recursion retains carrier order. Its empty base returns []; each successful head test either prepends that head or omits it, and any evaluated error propagates. Return-row construction accepts the item only if K_e(a)=[b]. With successful later rows R it prepends (a,b), so projection onto first coordinates reproduces the original item list by induction. An empty or multi-element candidate list returns None at that step.

For commutation, evaluate l=μ(a,b) and then r=μ(b,a). The cell returns true exactly when l=r. Applying the checked-traversal equivalence twice gives exhaustive agreement of all ordered pair cells. The candidate and commutation calculations use the retained table operation; neither replaces it with a named algebraic law.


40. Public return and commutation semantics

The return-mate API produces a successful row list if and only if its identity test succeeds and every carrier member has an exhaustive candidate search containing exactly one mate. Successful output has the same carrier projection in the same order. Every returned mate belongs to the carrier, returns to identity on both sides, and is the only carrier member satisfying both equations for that item.

For accepted operation tables, the commutation test succeeds if and only if the actual operation outputs agree for every ordered pair and its reversal. The constructor’s totality supplies both outputs in the converse direction, so the equivalence does not rely on vacuous comparisons of missing operations.

Let Success(e) mean that returnMates produces ok(Some(R)) for some row list R. Its initial branch requires hasIdentity(e)=ok(true). Row recursion then gives
  Success(e) ⇔ hasIdentity(e)=ok(true) and ∀a∈C, ∃b, returnCandidates(e,a)=ok([b]).
For the forward implication, the successful row projection contains every original a, and its corresponding row supplies the singleton search. For the reverse implication, take the mate supplied for the head, construct later rows by induction on the tail, and prepend the resulting pair. The empty tail closes with Some([]).

For a returned (a,b), singleton membership and the candidate-filter equivalence give b∈C and μ(a,b)=e=μ(b,a). Any other c∈C satisfying both equations belongs to the same singleton [b], hence c=b. Thus uniqueness follows from exhaustive singleton filtering. Similarly, on an accepted table, total lookup supplies μ(a,b) and μ(b,a) for every pair. Substituting these values into the cell equivalence proves
  isCommutative=ok(true) ⇔ ∀a,b∈C, μ(a,b)=μ(b,a).
Totality is used in the reverse implication to construct the successful comparisons, not merely to rule out counterexamples.


41. Operation-preserving maps

The mapping relation is converted to the source dictionary semantics: the first occurrence fixes a key’s position, and subsequent occurrences replace its value. Native proofs establish replacement lookup, preservation of other lookups and the last occurrence’s value. The preservation operation retains the source sequence of checks: dictionary cardinality, presence of every source origin with a target-carrier image, then the operation equation for every ordered source pair.

Each equation compares the image of the source operation result with the target operation applied to the two input images, in the source evaluation order. Successful preservation is equivalent to all original checks passing; each successful cell comparison is equivalent to equality of its actual results. Fresh execution agrees on 1555 complete dictionary comparisons and 6220 preservation calls across repeated-key, extra-key and external-image mappings. Dictionary invariants and accepted-table totality are established below.

Write put(D,a,v) for the source dictionary update and lookup(D,a) for its optional lookup. Structural induction on D gives
  lookup(put(D,a,v),a)=Some(v),
  lookup(put(D,a,v),b)=lookup(D,b) for b≠a.
At a matching key, replacement leaves its position unchanged; if no key matches, recursion appends the new pair. Processing concatenated mapping lists satisfies run(D,U++V)=run(run(D,U),V). Therefore a final occurrence (a,v) fixes lookup(build(U++[(a,v)]),a)=Some(v), preserving last-value semantics rather than rejecting repeated keys.

Let D be the built dictionary and f its lookup value where defined. The first two preservation guards require |D|=|C_source| and, for every a∈C_source, a defined f(a)∈C_target. For each ordered source pair, the cell evaluates in order
  u=μ_source(a,b), f(u), f(a), f(b), v=μ_target(f(a),f(b)),
and then compares f(u) with v. Given these successful lookups, the cell is true exactly when
  f(μ_source(a,b))=μ_target(f(a),f(b)).
The two nested checked traversals turn successful preservation into the conjunction of the original cardinality guard, image-membership guard and this equality at every source pair. Missing dictionary entries and operation failures retain their source error path and evaluation order.


42. Total structure-map semantics

Dictionary updates preserve distinct keys, and the complete dictionary construction therefore has one entry per retained origin. Lookup succeeds exactly at a stored key. The source image-carrier check is equivalent to successful lookup of an image in the target carrier.

For accepted source and target operation tables, those image checks supply the mapped inputs and the image of every source operation result; constructor closure supplies membership of the intermediate source result. Each preservation comparison consequently terminates with a Boolean result rather than a missing-key or operation error. The public preservation operation succeeds exactly when its original size and image checks hold and the image of every source operation result equals the corresponding target operation result.

Let keys(D) project dictionary origins. If its keys are distinct, put(D,a,v) either replaces an existing value without changing keys or appends a previously absent key. Induction over the input relation therefore gives distinct(keys(build(mapping))). Lookup induction gives
  (∃v, lookup(D,a)=Some(v)) ⇔ a∈keys(D).
Unfolding the image guard gives
  imageInCarrier(D,T,a)=true ⇔ ∃v∈T, imageAt(D,a)=ok(v).
For a,b∈C_source, accepted-table totality produces u=μ_source(a,b)∈C_source. The image guard supplies f(u),f(a),f(b), with f(a),f(b)∈C_target. Target totality then produces μ_target(f(a),f(b)). Consequently every checked cell terminates with the Boolean comparison f(u)=μ_target(f(a),f(b)). Substitution in the exhaustive pair traversal yields the exact public equivalence
  preservesOperation=ok(true) ⇔
  |D|=|C_source| and (∀a∈C_source, f(a) exists in C_target)
  and (∀a,b∈C_source, f(μ_source(a,b))=μ_target(f(a),f(b))).
The dictionary and table facts supply each value used in this equality.


43. Original algebra witnesses and complete grammar

Both original operation tables are retained and accepted by the translated constructor. The six registered witnesses are proved from their actual computations: complete four-row coverage, structural One identity, exhaustive association, exact two-sided return mates, commutation of the symmetric table together with failure of commutation for the lawful left-projection table, and preservation by the canonical self-map. All original names and statements are retained.

The algebra grammar contains all 512 products of its nine source dimensions. Native proofs establish exhaustive membership, exact cardinality, the unique all-preserving survivor, equivalence of acceptance to every original criterion, and rejection of each inadmissible coordinate. Fresh execution matches every candidate record’s order, identifier, survivor decision, exact form and reason against the pinned source artifacts. These finite grammar proofs accompany the general operation derivations and complete-program correspondence. The algebra entry is recorded complete against its registered V3 source.

Abbreviate the witness labels One and held by O and H. Its four ordered cells are
  O·O=O, O·H=H, H·O=H, H·H=O.
These cover all pairs and stay in {O,H}; O preserves both labels. In the ordered triple sequence OOO, OOH, OHO, OHH, HOO, HOH, HHO, HHH, both parenthesizations evaluate respectively to
  O, H, H, O, H, O, O, H.
Thus association is checked on all eight triples. The mate searches give K_O(O)=[O] and K_O(H)=[H], producing [(O,O),(H,H)]. The table is symmetric. By contrast, the second lawful table has a·a=a, a·b=a, b·a=b, b·b=b; for its distinct labels a·b=a≠b=b·a, so commutation fails. The self-map f(O)=O,f(H)=H satisfies f(x·y)=f(x)·f(y) on every cell by direct substitution. These computations establish the six original witness predicates.

The nine binary grammar axes generate 2⁹=512 ordered tuples. Acceptance is equivalent to the conjunction of the nine admitted-coordinate conditions. The all-preserving tuple satisfies that conjunction; any accepted tuple equals it coordinate by coordinate. A rejected value falsifies its coordinate condition, establishing the exclusion proofs while retaining the full product enumeration.


44. Algebra program correspondence

The translated algebra program retains its original registration and dependencies, all 512 candidate records and lookup decisions, completeness certificate, closure and generality payloads, and four original controls. Candidate lookup depends only on the registered identifier, and an unknown identifier retains the source StopIteration outcome. Fresh native execution reproduces the pinned source artifacts, including every census trace identity, decision proof identity, closure and generality certificate, control receipt and registration field. These correspondence results accompany the general operation, return and map derivations above. The registered algebra entry and its dependencies are complete in the coverage ledger.

45. Algebraic induction base and carrier extension

The One carrier has exactly its self pair. Its closed operation returns One, and both identity and association hold. More generally, closure and complete pair coverage force the sole result of any accepted singleton carrier to be its member; identity and association follow for every such accepted table.

Appending a fresh member retains the original nested-loop order of pair generation. Each existing left-member row gains its cell with the fresh right member, followed by the fresh left-member row and the fresh self cell. The enlarged support partitions into old pairs, old-to-fresh pairs, fresh-to-old pairs and the self pair. Exactly the last three classes are new, and a distinct original carrier with a fresh added member generates a distinct complete pair support. These results establish the base and pair-generation component of the original algebra induction; the property checks on an extended operation remain explicit obligations.

For a singleton carrier [o], pair support is [(o,o)]. Accepted-table totality and closure give μ(o,o)∈[o], forcing μ(o,o)=o. Hence o·o=o establishes both identity equations, and both association branches reduce to μ(μ(o,o),o)=μ(o,μ(o,o))=o.

Let z∉C. Distributing the ordered pair generator over C++[z] gives the exact list equation
  P(C++[z])=
    concat_(a∈C)([(a,b):b∈C]++[(a,z)])
    ++[(z,b):b∈C]++[(z,z)].
This preserves the interleaving of each old row with its new final cell. Pair membership consequently separates into four cases:
  (a∈C and b∈C), (a∈C and b=z),
  (a=z and b∈C), or (a=z and b=z).
Freshness makes these cases disjoint. Excluding membership in P(C) leaves exactly the final three cases, so no new pair is omitted and no old pair is reclassified as new. Distinctness of C and freshness of z give distinct(C++[z]); applying pair-product distinctness proves the enlarged support has no duplicate pairs. This constructs the complete extension support; the chosen outputs and their property checks are handled by the subsequent extension results.


46. Identity and association under carrier extension

The exhaustive check on an appended domain evaluates the original prefix first. A false result or exception stops that evaluation; only a successful prefix proceeds to the added domain. Identity on an enlarged carrier requires preservation of each old member and of the added member on both sides. When the old identity holds and all old operation cells are preserved, the enlarged identity holds exactly when its two equations on the added member hold.

Every enlarged-carrier triple either consists entirely of old members or contains the added member in at least one position. For an accepted old table, closure keeps both intermediate old pair results in the old carrier. Preserving old operation cells therefore preserves the full composed old triple calculations. If the old table is associative, the enlarged table is associative exactly when every triple containing the added member passes its original association check. All seven new-coordinate combinations are included; no enumeration or scientific guard is removed.


Write the old carrier list as C, the appended label as z, and C' = C ++ [z]. Write μ and μ' for the old and extended operations, with μ'(a,b) = μ(a,b) for a,b ∈ C. Each equality here denotes successful lookup as well as equality of the resulting label. The exhaustive Boolean check A satisfies

\[
 A(P,C\mathbin{+\!+}[z])=
 \begin{cases}
 \operatorname{error}(e),&A(P,C)=\operatorname{error}(e),\\
 \operatorname{ok}(\mathrm{false}),&A(P,C)=\operatorname{ok}(\mathrm{false}),\\
 P(z),&A(P,C)=\operatorname{ok}(\mathrm{true}).
 \end{cases}
\]

If e is an old two-sided identity, preservation of old cells gives μ'(e,a) = a = μ'(a,e) for every a ∈ C. Thus

\[
 e\text{ is an identity on }C'
 \iff \mu'(e,z)=z\ \land\ \mu'(z,e)=z.
\]

For old a,b,c, closure gives u = μ(a,b) ∈ C and v = μ(b,c) ∈ C. Consequently the intermediate and outer lookups are all preserved:

\[
 \mu'(\mu'(a,b),c)=\mu'(u,c)=\mu(u,c),\qquad
 \mu'(a,\mu'(b,c))=\mu'(a,v)=\mu(a,v).
\]

The old associativity equality therefore transfers. Expanding each coordinate of C' gives the old block C×C×C and seven remaining blocks:

\[
 (z,C,C),\ (C,z,C),\ (C,C,z),\ (z,z,C),\
 (z,C,z),\ (C,z,z),\ (z,z,z).
\]

Here each displayed triple denotes the corresponding Cartesian block of labels, retaining list enumeration. Under old associativity and closure, associativity on C' is therefore equivalent to

\[
 \forall a,b,c\in C',\quad
 (a=z\lor b=z\lor c=z)\Longrightarrow
 \mu'(\mu'(a,b),c)=\mu'(a,\mu'(b,c)).
\]

Every required lookup must succeed; an error is not an equality witness.


47. Return mates under carrier extension

The exhaustive mate filter preserves carrier order and propagates lookup errors. With old operation cells retained, each old member’s enlarged search consists of its original candidate search followed by the added candidate’s two-sided return test. An originally unique mate remains the only mate precisely when that last test returns false. A passing added candidate creates a second match, and an error propagates.

Given prior successful return mates and preservation of old cells, successful return mates on the enlarged carrier are equivalent to three obligations: both identity equations hold for the added member; its return test fails for every old member; and its own exhaustive search over the enlarged carrier has exactly one mate. The original identity precheck and singleton requirement remain intact.


Define R_e(a,b) as the checked predicate μ(a,b) = e and μ(b,a) = e, and let F_C(a) be the ordered list obtained by filtering C with this predicate. Primes denote the extended operation and carrier. For a ∈ C, preservation of old cells gives R'_e(a,b) = R_e(a,b) for every old b. Hence, after a successful old search,

\[
 F_{C'}(a)=
 \begin{cases}
 F_C(a)\mathbin{+\!+}[z],&R'_e(a,z)=\operatorname{ok}(\mathrm{true}),\\
 F_C(a),&R'_e(a,z)=\operatorname{ok}(\mathrm{false}),\\
 \operatorname{error}(u),&R'_e(a,z)=\operatorname{error}(u).
 \end{cases}
\]

An old search error propagates before the appended test is reached. If F_C(a) = [m_a], the successful alternatives have lengths 2 and 1 respectively. Therefore

\[
 \exists m\;F_{C'}(a)=[m]
 \iff R'_e(a,z)=\operatorname{ok}(\mathrm{false}),
\]

and in the successful case the retained mate is exactly m_a. This is a singleton-list condition, preserving the original enumeration and multiplicities. Combining this equation for every old a with the identity result of section 46 yields

\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \operatorname{ReturnSuccess}(C',e)\iff{}&
 [\mu'(e,z)=z\land\mu'(z,e)=z]\\
 &\land[\forall a\in C,\ R'_e(a,z)=\operatorname{ok}(\mathrm{false})]\\
 &\land[\exists m,\ F_{C'}(z)=[m]].
 \end{aligned}
\]

This equivalence assumes prior successful return mates and preserved old cells. The last search still examines all of C', including the appended candidate.


48. Operation maps under carrier extension

The original map-admission checks split into dictionary cardinality, images for old members and the added member, and operation preservation for old and newly formed source pairs. All left, right and self pairs involving the added member remain required.

When old source and target operation cells and old dictionary images are retained, source closure ensures that the old operation result also retains its image. Consequently the complete old preservation-cell calculation is unchanged. Old image coverage transfers when the enlarged target retains its old carrier. Given prior map success and these preservation conditions, enlarged map success is equivalent to the original dictionary-size guard, target membership of the added member’s image, and every new source-pair preservation check.


Let C,D be the old source and target carriers, μ,ν their operations, and f the dictionary produced by the original mapping constructor. The extended objects are C' = C ++ [z], D', μ',ν',f'. The admission condition is

\[
 |f'|=|C'|,\qquad
 \forall a\in C',\ f'(a)\in D',\qquad
 \forall a,b\in C',\ f'(\mu'(a,b))=\nu'(f'(a),f'(b)).
\]

The size is the number of entries in the constructed dictionary, not the length of an unprocessed mapping input. All images and operation lookups in the displayed equations must exist. Suppose the old table was accepted, the old map passed, D ⊆ D', and the extended operations and images agree on their old domains. For a,b ∈ C, closure places r = μ(a,b) in C. Thus

\[
 \begin{aligned}
 f'(\mu'(a,b))&=f'(r)=f(r)=f(\mu(a,b)),\\
 \nu'(f'(a),f'(b))&=\nu'(f(a),f(b))=\nu(f(a),f(b)).
 \end{aligned}
\]

The old map equality identifies the two final expressions, so all old pair checks transfer. Likewise f'(a) = f(a) ∈ D ⊆ D' transfers old image coverage. Splitting C'×C' leaves the old block and (C,z), (z,C), (z,z). Consequently the remaining necessary and sufficient checks are

\[
 \begin{aligned}
 &|f'|=|C'|,\qquad f'(z)\in D',\\
 &\forall a,b\in C',\ (a=z\lor b=z)\Longrightarrow
 f'(\mu'(a,b))=\nu'(f'(a),f'(b)).
 \end{aligned}
\]

This reduction preserves the original size guard, image coverage and every newly formed pair; it follows by transferring the old checks, not by dropping them.


49. Concrete dictionary extension

For a successful operation map from an accepted source table, the generated dictionary keys are a permutation of the source carrier. A fresh source member therefore has no existing dictionary entry. The original update operation appends its key and image at the end, increasing cardinality by one and preserving every old lookup. The fresh lookup returns its assigned image.

These facts discharge the dictionary-size and old-image preservation conditions for the concrete appended mapping. With the old source and target operations retained and the old target carrier included, that mapping preserves the enlarged operation exactly when the fresh image belongs to the enlarged target and every new source pair passes the operation equation. The dictionary’s original first-key order and last-value replacement behavior are retained.


Let I = buildImages(M) for the original mapping list M. Successful map admission gives |I| = |C| and a defined image for every a ∈ C. Since the accepted carrier is distinct, coverage and equal length imply

\[
 \operatorname{keys}(I)\sim C,
\]

where ∼ denotes list permutation. For z ∉ C, membership preservation under permutation gives z ∉ keys(I), hence lookup(I,z) = none. The original update therefore takes its absent-key branch:

\[
 I'=\operatorname{buildImages}(M\mathbin{+\!+}[(z,v)])
   =I\mathbin{+\!+}[(z,v)],\qquad
 |I'|=|I|+1=|C|+1=|C\mathbin{+\!+}[z]|.
\]

For every a ∈ C, freshness implies a ≠ z, so lookup(I',a) = lookup(I,a); meanwhile lookup(I',z) = some(v). Substitution into section 48 discharges the size and old-image obligations and gives

\[
 \operatorname{MapSuccess}(M\mathbin{+\!+}[(z,v)])\iff
 v\in D'\ \land\
 \forall a,b\in C',\ (a=z\lor b=z)\Longrightarrow
 f'(\mu'(a,b))=\nu'(f'(a),f'(b)).
\]

This uses the accepted old source, successful old map, preserved old source and target cells, and inclusion D ⊆ D'. Repeated keys elsewhere retain the original replacement rule; freshness is used only for this appended key.


50. Finite order and bound operations

The order kernel retains the original nonempty distinct carrier and distinct relation checks, rejects external endpoints, and exhaustively evaluates reflexivity, antisymmetry and transitivity. Totality requires both partial-order success and comparability of every generated carrier pair. The latter equivalence is proved natively.

Lower and upper bounds retain the nonempty held-selection guard and reject external held members. Successful results contain exactly the carrier members satisfying every held comparison, in original carrier order. Greatest and least witnesses are selected only when the complete matching list has exactly one entry. Meet and join retain the bound-selection errors and conditional extremal result. Monotonicity retains dictionary replacement semantics and checks both endpoint images and every source comparison.

Fresh execution matches 1404 order, bound and extremal records and 13932 monotonicity comparisons, covering all two-member relations together with malformed carriers/relations, repeated and outside selections, and repeated-key mappings. These execution results accompany the general partial-order and extremal proofs. The complete 512-candidate program comparison also matches the pinned V3 registration, census, decisions, closure, controls and unknown-identifier behavior.


Write a ≤_R b for (a,b) ∈ R. For a held selection H with H ≠ [] and every member in C, the actual bound lists are the ordered filters

\[
 L_R(H)=[x\in C\mid\forall h\in H,\ x\le_R h],\qquad
 U_R(H)=[x\in C\mid\forall h\in H,\ h\le_R x].
\]

The bracket notation denotes list filtering, retaining carrier order and multiplicity. Their membership equivalences follow directly from filter membership and the finite all-test. For a bound list B, form the complete extremal lists

\[
 G_R(B)=[g\in B\mid\forall b\in B,\ b\le_R g],\qquad
 E_R(B)=[e\in B\mid\forall b\in B,\ e\le_R b].
\]

The greatest selector returns some(g) exactly when G_R(B) = [g]; the least selector similarly requires E_R(B) = [e]. Thus, after successful bound selection,

\[
 \operatorname{meet}(C,R,H)=\operatorname{some}(m)
 \iff G_R(L_R(H))=[m],\qquad
 \operatorname{join}(C,R,H)=\operatorname{some}(j)
 \iff E_R(U_R(H))=[j].
\]

An empty or outside held selection returns the original error before extremal selection. Total order is also conditional:

\[
 \operatorname{Total}(C,R)\iff\operatorname{Partial}(C,R)
 \land\forall a,b\in C,\ (a\le_R b\lor b\le_R a).
\]

For a monotone map f from source relation R to target relation S, the comparison condition is (a,b) ∈ R ⇒ (f(a),f(b)) ∈ S, with the original dictionary and endpoint-image admission checks retained. No comparison absent from R is supplied by the map check.


51. Exact partial-order admission

For arbitrary represented finite carrier and relation lists, the partial-order check succeeds if and only if the carrier is nonempty and distinct, the relation is distinct, every relation endpoint belongs to the carrier, every carrier member has its reflexive cell, every pair of reversed relation cells has equal endpoints, and every pair of composable relation cells has its composite in the relation.

The native equivalence follows each original guard and exhaustive traversal. The transitivity condition covers all pairs of relation cells with matching middle endpoints; nonmatching cells impose no composite requirement. The reverse implication establishes success from precisely these same conditions.


With Distinct denoting absence of repeated list entries, the complete admission equivalence is

\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \operatorname{Partial}(C,R)\iff{}&C\ne[]\land\operatorname{Distinct}(C)
 \land\operatorname{Distinct}(R)\\
 &\land[\forall(a,b)\in R,\ a\in C\land b\in C]\\
 &\land[\forall a\in C,\ (a,a)\in R]\\
 &\land[\forall a,b,\ (a,b)\in R\land(b,a)\in R\Rightarrow a=b]\\
 &\land[\forall a,b,c,\ (a,b)\in R\land(b,c)\in R\Rightarrow(a,c)\in R].
 \end{aligned}
\]

For each finite traversal the elementary equivalence is

\[
 \operatorname{all}(P,[x_1,\ldots,x_n])=\mathrm{true}
 \iff\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}P(x_i)=\mathrm{true}.
\]

Applying it to carrier cells proves the reflexive clause. Applying it twice to relation cells proves antisymmetry and transitivity. In the latter traversal, a pair of cells (a,b),(u,c) passes automatically when b ≠ u; when b = u, its check is exactly (a,c) ∈ R. Therefore every composable pair is tested, and the displayed transitive clause supplies precisely the checks needed for the reverse implication. Combining these traversal equivalences with the three initial guards and endpoint check proves both directions of admission.


52. Extremal witnesses and returned bounds

Greatest and least operations return a value exactly when the complete filtered list of matching candidates is the singleton containing that value. This characterization retains occurrence multiplicity on unrestricted input lists. Every returned value belongs to the supplied bounds, dominates them in the appropriate direction, and is the only bound with that property. For distinct bounds, those semantic conditions also imply successful return.

Meet succeeds exactly when lower-bound generation succeeds and its complete result has the returned unique greatest witness; join has the corresponding upper-bound and unique least criterion. Every returned meet belongs to the carrier, lies below all held members, and is above every carrier lower bound. Every returned join belongs to the carrier, lies above all held members, and is below every carrier upper bound. Bound-generation errors and absence or multiplicity of matching extrema retain the original outcomes.


Using the ordered extremal filters G_R and E_R of section 50, successful greatest selection gives G_R(B) = [g]. Filter membership then yields

\[
 g\in B,\qquad\forall b\in B,\ b\le_R g,\qquad
 \forall r\in B,\ (\forall b\in B,\ b\le_R r)\Rightarrow r=g.
\]

Indeed, any such rival r belongs to G_R(B), and membership of [g] forces r = g. The least case reverses both comparisons. Conversely, if B is distinct, filtering preserves distinctness; membership of g together with exclusion of every different rival forces the entire matching list to be [g]. Distinctness matters here: two occurrences of the same matching label produce a two-entry list and the original selector returns none.

For successful lower-bound generation B = L_R(H), substitute its membership equivalence into the greatest result:

\[
 m\in C,\quad\forall h\in H,\ m\le_R h,\quad
 \forall x\in C,\ (\forall h\in H,\ x\le_R h)\Rightarrow x\le_R m.
\]

The dual substitution for B = U_R(H) gives

\[
 j\in C,\quad\forall h\in H,\ h\le_R j,\quad
 \forall x\in C,\ (\forall h\in H,\ h\le_R x)\Rightarrow j\le_R x.
\]

These conclusions follow from actual returned witnesses. They do not assume that an extremum exists for every held selection.


53. Conditional meet/join existence and monotonicity

The original bound guard succeeds exactly for a nonempty held selection whose members belong to the carrier. Bound generation then returns its full ordered filter. On a distinct carrier, both bound lists are distinct. For an accepted partial order and a valid held selection, meet returns a given value if and only if it is a carrier lower bound above every other carrier lower bound. Join has the dual equivalence. Antisymmetry supplies extremal uniqueness, so the converses imply actual successful evaluation.

Monotonicity succeeds exactly when both endpoint images exist for every source comparison and their ordered image pair belongs to the target relation. Dictionary replacement semantics are retained; an empty source relation passes its exhaustive check.

The six original witnesses are proved using their actual operations and original names/statements: the diamond is a partial order, its arms remain incomparable, the chain is total while the diamond is not, the arms meet at One and join at whole, and the canonical identity mapping preserves every diamond comparison. Fresh execution reproduces all six witness rows.


Assume Partial(C,R), H ≠ [], and H ⊆ C. The bound guard then returns its ordered filter, and distinctness of C implies distinctness of that filter. If m is a greatest lower bound and r is another greatest member of L_R(H), then

\[
 r\le_R m\quad\text{and}\quad m\le_R r
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad r=m
\]

by antisymmetry. Thus the greatest-filter list is exactly [m] and evaluation returns ok(some(m)). The least-upper-bound proof is dual. Together with section 52 this establishes

\[
 \operatorname{meet}(C,R,H)=\operatorname{ok}(\operatorname{some}(m))
 \iff m\text{ satisfies the displayed greatest-lower-bound conditions},
\]

and the analogous join equivalence. No existence is asserted when those conditions have no witness.

For the actual mapping dictionary I, monotonicity has the exact finite interpretation

\[
 \forall(a,b)\in R,\ \exists u,v,\
 \operatorname{lookup}(I,a)=\operatorname{some}(u)\land
 \operatorname{lookup}(I,b)=\operatorname{some}(v)\land(u,v)\in S.
\]

A missing endpoint image makes its cell false; an empty R has no cells to fail. The original diamond uses C = [One,left,right,whole] and

\[
 R=\{(x,x):x\in C\}\cup
 \{(One,left),(One,right),(One,whole),(left,whole),(right,whole)\}.
\]

Neither (left,right) nor (right,left) occurs. Direct complete bound selection gives

\[
 L_R([left,right])=[One],\qquad U_R([left,right])=[whole],
\]

so the corresponding extremal filters return meet One and join whole. The three-member chain has the reflexive cells and (a,b),(b,c),(a,c), giving comparability of every pair. Finally the identity dictionary satisfies (f(a),f(b)) = (a,b) ∈ R for each relation cell, proving the sixth witness directly.


54. Complete order grammar and program correspondence

The order/lattice grammar retains all 512 combinations of its nine original dimensions: carrier identity, relation provenance, partial-order witnesses, comparability, bound generation, meet uniqueness, join uniqueness, monotonicity and extra-order status. Native proofs establish complete membership, cardinality, exact acceptance criteria, the sole all-admitted survivor and rejection of each inadmissible coordinate. Every combination remains in the source order.

The native program retains its original registration and dependencies, complete candidate records, identifier lookup behavior, completeness and trace identities, decision proof identities, closure and generality payloads, and four original controls. Fresh execution matches all 512 candidate records and complete program fields against the pinned source artifacts. Unknown identifiers retain StopIteration. These results accompany the general order, bound, monotonicity and carrier-extension derivations for the completed order/lattice entry. Completion of this entry is distinct from completion of the entire SFT formalisation.


Let A_i be the ordered two-choice list for source dimension i, with rejected choice followed by admitted choice. The complete candidate list is the ordered Cartesian product

\[
 \Gamma=A_1\times\cdots\times A_9,\qquad
 |\Gamma|=\prod_{i=1}^{9}|A_i|=2^9=512.
\]

Every typed candidate has one member of each A_i and therefore belongs to Γ. For a candidate c, let p_i(c) assert that its ith coordinate is the original admitted choice. Exhaustive evaluation establishes

\[
 \operatorname{survives}(c)=\operatorname{criteriaDecision}(c),\qquad
 \operatorname{criteriaDecision}(c)=\mathrm{true}
 \iff\bigwedge_{i=1}^{9}p_i(c)
 \iff c=c_*.
\]

Here c_* is the tuple of all nine admitted choices. A rejected coordinate contradicts the corresponding conjunct, so no candidate with any rejected coordinate survives. These equations describe the complete registered grammar; the order-operation derivations in sections 50–53 establish the mathematical content tested by its admitted choices. The program comparison checks all 512 source-ordered records and their accompanying fields, rather than sampling only c_*.


55. Order induction base and comparison extension

The One carrier with its identity relation is both a partial and total order. Its complete lower and upper bound lists for the held One selection are the singleton One, and both meet and join return One.

For a fresh carrier addition preserving every old comparison cell, the original endpoint and distinct-relation guards are retained. Partial-order success on the enlarged carrier is equivalent to the new reflexive cell, antisymmetry for every reversed pair touching the fresh member, and closure for every composable triple touching it. Old-only obligations follow from the prior order and retained comparisons. Given prior totality and an admitted enlarged partial order, totality of the enlarged order is equivalent to comparison between the fresh member and each old member.


The base carrier and relation are C₁ = [One] and R₁ = [(One,One)]. Their single relation cell simultaneously supplies reflexivity, antisymmetry, transitivity and comparability. Direct filtering gives

\[
 L_{R_1}([One])=U_{R_1}([One])=[One],\qquad
 \operatorname{meet}=\operatorname{join}=\operatorname{ok}(\operatorname{some}(One)).
\]

For C' = C ++ [z], assume Partial(C,R), z ∉ C, distinct R', all endpoints of R' in C', and exact retention (a,b) ∈ R' ⇔ (a,b) ∈ R for old a,b. Then

\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \operatorname{Partial}(C',R')\iff{}&(z,z)\in R'\\
 &\land[\forall a,b,\ (a,b),(b,a)\in R'\land(a=z\lor b=z)\Rightarrow a=b]\\
 &\land[\forall a,b,c,\ (a,b),(b,c)\in R'\land
 (a=z\lor b=z\lor c=z)\Rightarrow(a,c)\in R'].
 \end{aligned}
\]

To prove the reverse direction, classify each endpoint as old or equal to z. All-old cells reduce through retention to the old laws; every remaining case is one of the displayed new checks. Freshness preserves carrier distinctness, while relation distinctness and endpoint admission remain explicit hypotheses. If the old order is total and the extended relation is already a partial order, the same split gives

\[
 \operatorname{Total}(C',R')\iff
 \forall a\in C,\ (z,a)\in R'\lor(a,z)\in R'.
\]

The old-old comparisons transfer, and (z,z) is supplied by extended reflexivity.


56. Bound lists under carrier extension

Successful bound generation establishes that the held selection is nonempty and contained in the original carrier. When every old comparison cell is retained and that held selection remains fixed, every old candidate has the same bound-check result in the enlarged relation.

The enlarged lower-bound list is exactly the original ordered list followed by the added member when it lies below every held member; otherwise the list is unchanged. The upper-bound list has the dual formula. These are equalities of the actual generated lists, preserving order and occurrence multiplicity, and require no additional distinctness assumption.


Let the held selection H be valid on C, and let R' retain all old comparisons. For every x ∈ C and h ∈ H, retention identifies both lower and upper comparison tests. Hence the complete old-candidate filters are unchanged. Using the list identity

\[
 \operatorname{filter}(P,C\mathbin{+\!+}[z])
 =\operatorname{filter}(P,C)\mathbin{+\!+}\operatorname{filter}(P,[z]),
\]

the actual extended bound lists are

\[
 L_{C',R'}(H)=L_{C,R}(H)\mathbin{+\!+}
 \begin{cases}[z],&\forall h\in H,\ z\le_{R'}h,\\\text{empty list},&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}
\]

\[
 U_{C',R'}(H)=U_{C,R}(H)\mathbin{+\!+}
 \begin{cases}[z],&\forall h\in H,\ h\le_{R'}z,\\\text{empty list},&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}
\]

The original successful bound result proves H ≠ [] and H ⊆ C ⊆ C', so the extended guard also succeeds. These formulas do not require freshness of z or distinctness of C: an appended repeated label remains an appended occurrence, exactly as in the source lists.


57. Held-selection updates and extremal retention

Adding a carrier member to an already valid held selection filters the existing lower-bound list by comparison below that member, and filters the upper-bound list by comparison above it. These are equalities of the generated lists, retaining their order and multiplicity. On an accepted partial order, the old meet remains the meet exactly when it lies below the added held member; the old join remains exactly when it lies above it.

For an admitted carrier extension that retains all old comparisons, an existing meet remains exactly when the added carrier member, if it is a lower bound, lies below that meet. The join condition is dual: an added upper bound must lie above the old join. The conditions preserve the original conditional existence of extrema and include all old carrier candidates through the complete prior bound properties.


For an added held member y ∈ C, the finite all-test splits as

\[
 [\forall h\in H\mathbin{+\!+}[y],\ x\le_R h]
 \iff[\forall h\in H,\ x\le_R h]\land x\le_R y.
\]

Consequently, for previously valid H,

\[
 L_R(H\mathbin{+\!+}[y])=[x\in L_R(H)\mid x\le_R y],\qquad
 U_R(H\mathbin{+\!+}[y])=[x\in U_R(H)\mid y\le_R x].
\]

Assume an accepted partial order and prior returned meet m and join j. If m ≤_R y, then m remains a lower bound of the enlarged held selection; every new lower bound was an old lower bound and therefore lies below m. Conversely any returned meet m must lie below y. The dual argument proves

\[
 \operatorname{meet}(H\mathbin{+\!+}[y])=\operatorname{ok}(\operatorname{some}(m))\iff m\le_R y,
\]

\[
 \operatorname{join}(H\mathbin{+\!+}[y])=\operatorname{ok}(\operatorname{some}(j))\iff y\le_R j.
\]

For carrier extension C' = C ++ [z], instead keep H fixed, retain all old comparisons, and assume Partial(C',R'). Every old lower-bound candidate is still bounded by m; only the appended candidate can add an obligation. Thus

\[
 \operatorname{meet}(C',R',H)=\operatorname{ok}(\operatorname{some}(m))
 \iff[(\forall h\in H,\ z\le_{R'}h)\Rightarrow z\le_{R'}m].
\]

Similarly,

\[
 \operatorname{join}(C',R',H)=\operatorname{ok}(\operatorname{some}(j))
 \iff[(\forall h\in H,\ h\le_{R'}z)\Rightarrow j\le_{R'}z].
\]

If z is not a bound, the corresponding implication is vacuous; if it is a bound, its comparison with the prior extremum is required. The accepted-order extremum equivalences of section 53 turn these complete bound conditions into actual successful returns.


58. Extremal selection of the added carrier member

For an accepted carrier extension retaining all original comparisons, the added member is selected as meet exactly when it lies below every held member and above every member of the original lower-bound list. Dually, it is selected as join exactly when it lies above every held member and below every original upper bound. The original bound lists arise from successful nonempty held selections. Every original bound participates in these conditions; reflexivity supplies the added member's self-comparison, and accepted partial-order antisymmetry supplies extremal uniqueness. No existence of an old meet or join is required.


Let B = L_(C,R)(H) be the successfully generated old lower-bound list, and let C' = C ++ [z] carry an accepted relation R' retaining all old comparisons. Then

\[
 \operatorname{meet}(C',R',H)=\operatorname{ok}(\operatorname{some}(z))
 \iff[\forall h\in H,\ z\le_{R'}h]\land[\forall b\in B,\ b\le_{R'}z].
\]

Necessity follows by applying the returned-meet conditions to every old lower bound. For sufficiency, any lower-bound candidate in C' is either old, hence belongs to B by retained comparisons, or is z itself. The displayed condition handles the former; reflexivity handles the latter. The accepted-order equivalence then yields the actual singleton extremum. Dually, for B = U_(C,R)(H),

\[
 \operatorname{join}(C',R',H)=\operatorname{ok}(\operatorname{some}(z))
 \iff[\forall h\in H,\ h\le_{R'}z]\land[\forall b\in B,\ z\le_{R'}b].
\]

Only successful old bound generation is assumed: B need not have had an extremum before the extension.


59. Generated graphs, complete paths and cuts

Graph construction checks a nonempty canonical node collection, duplicate-free edge relations and membership of every edge endpoint in the carrier, in that order. Path validation retains the nonempty path, every node membership and every adjacent ordered edge. The recursively generated adjacency list is proved equal to the original path zipped with its tail. The identity path is admitted exactly for a node in the carrier.

At a shared interface node, the adjacency list of the composed path equals the complete first adjacency list followed by the complete second adjacency list. Consequently the composed path is valid if and only if both component paths are valid. This is an equivalence for arbitrary finite prefixes and suffixes, without erasing any transition.

A successful cut returns exactly the original edge list filtered by unequal held-endpoint membership, preserving edge order and multiplicity. Its membership criterion is proved directly. The internal balance operation is equivalent to nonempty ingress, equal ingress and egress trace counts, and separate distinctness of both trace collections. The executable criterion is retained exactly.

Fresh native execution matches 4597 independently reconstructed records for graph construction, paths, adjacency, cuts and balance, including malformed and repeated inputs. The complete native GraphProgram also matches the pinned V3 registration, all 512 candidate decisions, census and decision hashes, closure, controls and unknown-identifier behavior.


For node list V and edge list E, successful construction requires

\[
 V\ne[],\quad\operatorname{Distinct}(V),\quad\operatorname{Distinct}(E),\quad
 \forall(u,v)\in E,\ u,v\in V.
\]

For a nonempty path P = [v₀,…,vₙ], the complete adjacent list is Adj(P) = [(v₀,v₁),…,(vₙ₋₁,vₙ)], equivalently zip(P,tail(P)). Path admission is exactly

\[
 P\ne[]\land(\forall v\in P,\ v\in V)\land
 (\forall e\in\operatorname{Adj}(P),\ e\in E).
\]

At interface z, take P = A ++ [z] and Q = z :: B. The concatenation retains z once, and the exact adjacency identity is

\[
 \operatorname{Adj}(A\mathbin{+\!+}(z::B))
 =\operatorname{Adj}(A\mathbin{+\!+}[z])\mathbin{+\!+}\operatorname{Adj}(z::B).
\]

Splitting node membership and the exhaustive edge check across this concatenation proves validity of the composite if and only if both components are valid. For a nonempty held selection H ⊆ V, the cut is the ordered filter

\[
 \operatorname{Cut}_H(E)=[(u,v)\in E\mid(u\in H)\mathbin{\mathrm{xor}}(v\in H)].
\]

Thus an edge occurs in the result exactly when it occurs in E and exactly one endpoint is held. Finally, for incoming and outgoing trace lists I,O, the original balance predicate is

\[
 I\ne[]\land |I|=|O|\land\operatorname{Distinct}(I)\land\operatorname{Distinct}(O).
\]

This is the complete executable predicate; no additional conservation condition is inserted.


60. Finite reachability search

Reachability retains the original identity case: equal origin and target return exactly origin membership in the node carrier. Otherwise the queue starts with the origin and the visited trace is empty. Each iteration removes the queue head, checks target identity before visited membership, skips a previously visited head, or appends a new head to visited and appends every matching unvisited edge target to the queue in original edge order.

Termination follows from finite support and queue length. Initially the support consists of the origin and every edge target. Every queued node and edge target lies in that support or in visited. A new visit removes the held node from the auxiliary unseen support, strictly decreasing its length. A visited skip leaves support unchanged and strictly shortens the queue. Their lexicographic order is well founded. The auxiliary support does not decide search success, change queue order, omit duplicate enqueues or impose a depth bound.

The native search satisfies the exact fixed-point equation and the original empty-queue, target-hit, visited-skip and fresh-expansion equations. Fresh execution matches 8,573 reachability decisions and 10,280 ordered outgoing lists across every directed relation on three nodes, with additional duplicate-edge and external-endpoint stress inputs. Cycle search and its full return-path correspondence are formalised below.


Let Q be the queue, S the visited list and t the target. Define Out_E(u,S) to be the ordered list of edge targets v for edges (u,v) ∈ E with v ∉ S. The exact recursive cases are

\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \operatorname{Search}([],S)&=\mathrm{false},\\
 \operatorname{Search}(u::Q,S)&=\mathrm{true}&&\text{if }u=t,\\
 &=\operatorname{Search}(Q,S)&&\text{if }u\ne t\text{ and }u\in S,\\
 &=\operatorname{Search}(Q\mathbin{+\!+}\operatorname{Out}_E(u,S\mathbin{+\!+}[u]),S\mathbin{+\!+}[u])
 &&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{aligned}
\]

The target test precedes the visited test. Initially Q = [origin] and S = []; equal origin and target are handled separately by origin ∈ V. Let U be the auxiliary unseen list, initially origin :: map(target,E). Coverage states that every queued node and every edge target lies in U or S. A fresh head u therefore belongs to U. Removing all its occurrences strictly decreases |U|; a visited skip leaves U unchanged and decreases |Q|. Each recursive call consequently decreases the lexicographic measure

\[
 (|U|,|Q|).
\]

Finite-list length makes this order well founded. Expansion still appends every source-ordered unvisited target, including duplicate enqueues; the auxiliary measure proves termination without changing the displayed search equations.


61. Complete path witnesses for successful reachability

For every graph admitted by the original constructor and every origin and target, a true reachability result entails a complete path witness. The witness is a finite node list whose first node is the origin, whose final node is the target, and which satisfies the original path validator. Thus it retains every node membership and every ordered adjacent edge. The theorem requires no separate assumption that the queried origin belongs to the carrier.

Outgoing-list membership is equivalent to a retained edge from the held node to the queried node together with that node's absence from visited. Every property of queued nodes that is preserved by each retained edge is preserved through the exact queue loop. Applying this invariant to complete path witnesses establishes search soundness. The initial origin is retained as an alternative until its first edge supplies carrier membership; the equal-origin/target case obtains membership directly from the original identity check. Successful construction supplies both endpoint memberships for every retained edge.

Path extension appends the new target to the complete prior path and uses the shared-interface composition theorem, preserving its origin and every earlier transition. The resulting proof covers arbitrary finite admitted graphs, rather than only the enumerated execution cases. Search completeness is established by the closed-set argument below.


For a graph G, define the complete witness predicate

\[
 \operatorname{PathWitness}_G(o,t)\iff
 \exists A,\ \operatorname{Valid}_G(A\mathbin{+\!+}[t])
 \land\operatorname{head}(A\mathbin{+\!+}[t])=o.
\]

The queue proof uses a property P closed under retained edges:

\[
 (u,v)\in E\land P(u)\Rightarrow P(v).
\]

If P holds for every queued node, a target hit establishes P(t). A visited skip preserves P on the shorter queue. A fresh expansion preserves it on the old remainder and every appended target by edge closure. Induction along the decreasing search measure therefore proves search success implies P(t).

Choose P(v) to mean v = o or PathWitness_G(o,v). This holds initially even if o has not separately been admitted as a node. On the first traversed edge (o,v), accepted graph endpoints supply o ∈ V and v ∈ V; the identity path [o] therefore extends to [o,v]. Every later edge extends the complete prior witness. If o ≠ t, the alternative t = o is impossible, leaving a path witness. If o = t, the original identity check itself supplies o ∈ V and the singleton witness. Thus

\[
 \operatorname{Reachable}_G(o,t)=\mathrm{true}
 \Longrightarrow\operatorname{PathWitness}_G(o,t)
\]

for every admitted graph and arbitrary queried endpoints.


62. Reachability completeness and equivalence

Every complete valid origin-to-target path is found by the original finite queue search. The proof preserves the invariant that every edge leaving a visited node ends either in visited or in the frontier. Removing an already visited queue head preserves that invariant. Expanding a new head preserves it because every unvisited outgoing target is appended, in the original edge order.

If search terminates unsuccessfully, the resulting exhausted finite node collection contains the initial frontier and visited nodes, excludes the target, and is closed under every retained edge. A complete adjacent path beginning inside such a collection remains inside it at every transition. Consequently a valid path to the target contradicts unsuccessful search. This establishes completeness without bounding path length or discarding repeated queue entries.

Combining completeness with soundness gives the exact equivalence, for every admitted finite graph: reachability returns true if and only if a complete valid path starts at the queried origin and ends at the queried target. Identity reachability holds exactly for carrier nodes. Complete path witnesses compose at their shared interface, yielding the original transitivity law for reachability.


The visited/frontier invariant is

\[
 u\in S\land(u,v)\in E\Longrightarrow v\in S\lor v\in Q.
\]

If a queue head is skipped, it already belongs to S, so removing that occurrence from Q preserves coverage. If a fresh head is expanded, it is appended to S and every outgoing target not in the new S is appended to Q. The displayed invariant is therefore preserved in both recursive cases.

Assuming the target is absent from S, unsuccessful search supplies a finite exhausted list X with

\[
 S\subseteq X,\qquad Q\subseteq X,\qquad t\notin X,\qquad
 u\in X\land(u,v)\in E\Rightarrow v\in X.
\]

At the initial state, o ∈ Q ⊆ X. Induction over adjacent cells of any complete valid o-to-t path propagates membership in X from its first node to its last, forcing t ∈ X, a contradiction. Hence every path witness is found. Combining both directions gives

\[
 \operatorname{Reachable}_G(o,t)=\mathrm{true}
 \iff\operatorname{PathWitness}_G(o,t).
\]

For admitted G, shared-interface path composition then proves

\[
 \operatorname{Reachable}_G(o,m)\land\operatorname{Reachable}_G(m,t)
 \Rightarrow\operatorname{Reachable}_G(o,t),
\]

while Reachable_G(v,v) is equivalent to v ∈ V. No bound on path length enters either implication.


63. Exact finite cycle traversal

The cycle procedure retains the original outer node order and recursively scans every edge in its original order. Edges whose origin differs from the current node are skipped. An edge to a node already in the held path returns true immediately. Otherwise its target is appended to the held path and visited recursively; a false result resumes scanning the remaining edges. No branch or edge is removed.

Finite termination follows from an auxiliary list containing every edge target. Every target remains either in that unseen support or in the held path. A recursive call is made only for a target absent from the held path; that target therefore occurs in unseen support, and removing it strictly decreases the support length. Each local edge scan is structurally recursive on the remaining edge list. Neither argument introduces a depth cutoff.

The native procedure satisfies its exact recursive equation. Empty edge sets and nodes without outgoing edges cannot yield a cycle, and a retained outgoing edge to a held-path member forces a true result. Fresh execution matches 3,084 graph-cycle decisions and 14,392 recursive visits across all directed relations on three nodes and additional malformed/repeated input stresses. General equivalence with complete nontrivial return paths is established below.


Let Visit(u,H) denote recursive cycle exploration from node u with ordered held path H, and Scan(u,H,L) its scan of the remaining ordered edge list L. With e = (a,b), the exact source cases are

\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \operatorname{Scan}(u,H,[])&=\mathrm{false},\\
 \operatorname{Scan}(u,H,e::L)&=\operatorname{Scan}(u,H,L)&&\text{if }a\ne u,\\
 &=\mathrm{true}&&\text{if }a=u\text{ and }b\in H,\\
 &=\operatorname{Visit}(b,H\mathbin{+\!+}[b])\lor\operatorname{Scan}(u,H,L)
 &&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{aligned}
\]

The final disjunction short-circuits in the displayed order: the scan resumes only when the recursive visit returns false. Each Visit scans the entire original E, and the outer result is

\[
 \operatorname{CycleExists}(G)=
 \operatorname{any}_{u\in V}\operatorname{Visit}(u,[u]),
\]

in original node order. For recursive calls, auxiliary coverage places every edge target in unseen U or H. Since a recursive target b is absent from H, it occurs in U; removing its occurrences gives |U'| < |U|. Local scan recursion instead shortens L. These two finite decreases establish termination while preserving every branch and the ordered held path. An empty E makes every scan false; a matching edge to H makes its scan true immediately.


64. Cycle detection and complete return paths

For every admitted finite graph, the original cycle procedure returns true if and only if there is a complete valid path with at least one edge whose final node occurs earlier in the path. The starting node belongs to the graph carrier; every node and adjacency is checked by the original path validator. Self-loops satisfy the one-edge case. Longer witnesses may contain repeated nodes, with no extra simplicity restriction.

The proof first establishes exact correspondence between the recursive traversal and a finite return trace recording every successive edge target. A trace either closes along a retained edge to an already held node, or advances along a retained edge to a new node and continues with that node appended to the held path. Every such trace is nonempty, contains only retained adjacent edges, and yields a valid node path on an admitted graph. Its terminal target belongs to the held path together with the preceding trace prefix.

Conversely, a complete path ending at a prior node produces a return trace. Traversal follows its retained edges until the first held-node return; if a repeated node occurs before the supplied terminal return, that earlier return supplies the witness. Thus neither the equivalence nor the executable traversal requires paths to be simple. The proof applies to arbitrary finite admitted graphs and does not depend on the finite execution comparisons.


The retained ReturnTrace relation has exactly two constructors. A closing edge (u,v) ∈ E with v ∈ H yields trace [v]. An advancing edge (u,v) ∈ E with v ∉ H followed by a return trace T from (v,H ++ [v]) yields v :: T. Induction over these constructors proves nonemptiness, every required adjacent edge, and a closing decomposition

\[
 T=A\mathbin{+\!+}[t],\qquad t\in H\mathbin{+\!+}A.
\]

Induction over the exact recursive scan proves it returns true exactly when such a trace exists. With H = [u], graph admission supplies all endpoint memberships and converts u :: T to a complete valid path. Thus the exact graph-level equivalence is

\[
 \operatorname{CycleExists}(G)=\mathrm{true}\iff
 \exists u,A,t,\ u\in V\land\operatorname{Valid}_G(u::(A\mathbin{+\!+}[t]))
 \land t\in u::A.
\]

The displayed path has at least one edge, including when A is empty. Conversely, follow its adjacent edges until the first target already in the held prefix. That edge supplies the closing constructor; all earlier edges supply advancing constructors. Any earlier repetition closes sooner and is still detected, so simplicity is neither assumed nor imposed.


65. Graph witnesses and complete registered grammar

The six original graph witnesses are retained with their exact names, statements and executable predicates: valid chain path, missing-edge rejection, directed reachability, explicit cycle return, exact crossing cut and paired internal balance. The chain and cycle examples satisfy the original graph constructor. Positive reachability and return results follow from the general path theorems; the negative chain cases follow from the exact traversal equations.

The registered graph grammar exhausts all 512 combinations of its nine binary structural coordinates: nodes, edges, orientation, path, connectivity, cycle, cut, balance and additional rules. Native proofs establish complete enumeration, cardinality, equality of source classification with the preservation criteria, the unique all-preserving survivor, and rejection when any coordinate is replaced by its source alternative. No candidate is omitted.

Fresh native execution of the complete graph program matches the pinned registration, census, all 512 actual candidate lookups and decisions, trace and proof hashes, closure and generality records, four original controls and unknown-identifier behavior. These results accompany the general path/search and cycle/return equivalences. The source graph induction results appear below; the graph entry and its complete native program correspondence are recorded as complete.


The chain witness uses V = [a,b,c], E = [(a,b),(b,c)]. Therefore Adj([a,b,c]) = E, whereas Adj([a,c]) = [(a,c)] is absent. Its concrete results are

\[
 \operatorname{Valid}([a,b,c])=\mathrm{true},\quad
 \operatorname{Valid}([a,c])=\mathrm{false},\quad
 \operatorname{Reachable}(a,c)=\mathrm{true},\quad
 \operatorname{Reachable}(c,a)=\mathrm{false}.
\]

The reverse search exhausts immediately because c has no outgoing edge. Appending (c,a) supplies the return path [a,b,c,a], so cycle detection is true on the augmented graph and false on the chain. Holding H = [a] gives Cut_H(E) = [(a,b)]. The balance witness uses I = [[i1],[i2]] and O = [[o1],[o2]]: both are distinct, I is nonempty and |I| = |O| = 2.

For the original nine binary grammar axes, Γ = A₁×⋯×A₉ has

\[
 |\Gamma|=2^9=512,\qquad
 \operatorname{survives}(c)=\mathrm{true}
